    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like my whole day gets swallowed up by checking things in the house. It’s the same stuff over and over—outlets, locks, tools, dust, little things that should be simple but somehow never feel settled.\n\nI’ll check an outlet, and then I’ll walk away, but almost right away I feel like I didn’t really check it properly, or I missed something, so I go back. Same with the locks—I can look right at them, touch them, make sure they’re set, but then a few minutes later I’m back there again. Tools have to be put just right, and if there’s dust or something out of place, I’ll fix it, then fix it again, and it just keeps going.\n\nBy the time I’m done, I’m exhausted, but the worst part is I still don’t feel completely sure. It’s like I can’t trust that it’s okay enough to leave alone, even when I’ve already spent hours on it. That’s really why I came in. It’s taking over most of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind and are disturbing or upsetting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of what makes me go back and check, I think. It’s not just that I’m being careful. It’s these thoughts that jump into my head, and they feel awful.\n\nLike, I’ll know I looked at something properly. I’ll know I tightened something or put something away or checked the outlet, but then this thought comes in like, what if there’s a loose wire I missed and the whole house burns down? And it’s not just a normal “what if,” it feels like I can almost picture it happening, like I can see the consequences of me not catching it. Or if I was using nails or tools, I’ll get this thought that maybe I left a nail somewhere and my wife is going to step on it or get hurt somehow because I was careless.\n\nI try to tell myself, no, I already checked, I did it right, but it doesn’t really stick. I try to push the thought away, and sometimes that almost makes it louder, like it comes back stronger and meaner. It keeps repeating until I feel like I have to do something about it, because if I don’t, I feel like I’m choosing to let something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. I’m not just checking for the sake of checking, even though I know from the outside it probably looks that way. In my head, I’m doing it because if I don’t, something could go wrong and it would be my fault.\n\nSo I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things around the house, and I keep checking them or fixing them because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. But the problem is it never really feels finished. I can check it, and then I’ll think, “Did I really see it? Did I miss something?” So I go back again.\n\nIt ends up taking most of my day sometimes. I get worn out from it, honestly, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but for me it’s less like everything has to look perfect and more like it has to feel settled in the right way. Like the tools can’t just be “put away,” they have to be in the spot where I can look at them and feel like, okay, nothing’s loose, nothing’s been left out, nothing’s going to cause a problem. And even then, a lot of the time I don’t get that feeling.\n\nI’ll go back over the same things again and again—the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little bits around the house—and I’ll check them or fix them, then stand there trying to decide if it’s okay enough. Sometimes I move something or wipe something and then I think, no, that still doesn’t seem right, or I’m not sure I really checked it. So I do it again.\n\nIt ends up taking up most of my day, honestly. I get worn out from going over the same places so many times, but even after all that I still don’t really feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be symmetrical or exactly in a certain position?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need one thing lined up perfectly with another thing, or everything has to look balanced. It’s more that I get stuck on whether it’s actually safe or done right.\n\nLike I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even little bits of dust or things around the house, and I’ll check them or fix them again and again. Sometimes something being a little out of place bothers me, but mostly because it makes me think I missed something, or that something isn’t secure, or that I didn’t really finish checking it properly.\n\nSo it can look like I’m arranging things, I guess, because I’ll move tools around or wipe an area or put something back a certain way, but I’m not trying to make it symmetrical. I’m trying to get that feeling of, “Okay, now I can leave it alone.” But I don’t really get there. I can spend most of the day going back over the same stuff until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to walk away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or fixing needs to be perfect, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: It’s more about preventing something bad from happening, I think. I mean, “perfect” is part of it in the sense that if it doesn’t feel right, I can’t leave it alone, but I’m not sitting there trying to make it look flawless or neat just for the sake of it.\n\nIt’s like… I’m here because I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust in the house, checking and fixing them again and again. I’ll think, okay, if I miss something, something could go wrong, or I’ll be responsible somehow. So I check it, then I start doubting whether I really checked it properly, and then I go back.\n\nBy the end of it I’m worn out, honestly. But even when I’m exhausted, I still don’t feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone. So it’s not really about wanting it perfect like decoration or order—it’s more like I’m trying to get enough certainty that nothing bad is going to happen, and I just can’t seem to get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check things repeatedly even if someone else, like a family member, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, even if somebody else said, “I already checked the lock,” or “the outlet’s fine,” it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might help for a second, maybe, but then I start thinking, what if they didn’t check it the way I would, or what if they missed the thing I’m worried about, or what if I didn’t actually see it myself.\n\nSo I’d still end up going back over it. The outlets, the locks, the tools, little dust or bits around the house… I know it probably sounds excessive, but I can spend most of my day checking and fixing the same things again and again. And even after I’ve done it so many times that I’m worn out, I still don’t get that sure feeling, like, “Okay, it’s safe, I can leave it alone now.”\n\nSo someone else checking doesn’t really stop it. I wish it did. But I usually still feel like I have to check it myself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or contamination when you're checking these things around the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, at least. I’m not usually thinking, like, “This is covered in germs,” or that I’m going to get sick from touching it. That doesn’t really feel like the main thing in my head.\n\nWhen I’m checking around the house, it’s more like I’m trying to make sure something is safe or finished or not going to cause a problem. If I notice dust or something small, I might get stuck on it, but I don’t know that I’d call it contamination. It’s more that it makes me wonder if I missed something or if the area wasn’t really dealt with properly.\n\nI mean, I do clean or wipe things sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m scared of germs exactly. It’s hard to explain because in the moment it just feels like something is “not right,” and then I have to go back to it. But germs specifically… no, I haven’t really noticed that being what I’m worried about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to perform these checks a specific number of times to feel assured?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific number, no. It’s not like I say, “I have to check the lock seven times,” and then I’m done. I wish it worked that way, honestly, because then at least there’d be an ending to it.\n\nIt’s more that I keep going back until I feel sure, but the problem is I don’t really get that sure feeling. So I’ll check an outlet, or a lock, or a tool, or something I noticed with dust or whatever in the house, and then I walk away and almost immediately start doubting it. Like, did I really look closely enough? Did I fix it right? Did I miss something? And then I’m back there again.\n\nSometimes I lose track of how many times I’ve done it because it’s just over and over. It can take up most of the day, and by the end I’m exhausted, but even then I still don’t feel fully assured enough to just leave it alone. So it’s not a number exactly—it’s more like I’m chasing this feeling that it’s finally okay, and I can’t seem to reach it.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking things repeatedly, is it more because you're afraid of making a mistake itself, or because you're afraid of what might happen as a result of that mistake?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of what might happen after, I think. I mean, I do hate the idea that I made a mistake, but the part that really grabs onto me is the consequence of it. Like even if I know, logically, that I checked it and I did the job right, these thoughts just jump in anyway.\n\nI’ll think, what if there’s a loose wire I somehow missed and it burns the house down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt? And it’s not like I want to think that. I try to push it away and tell myself, no, I already checked, it’s fine, but then it comes back even stronger, like my brain is saying, “Are you sure? Are you absolutely sure?”\n\nSo it’s not just, “Oh, I don’t want to be wrong.” It’s more like… if I’m wrong, something terrible could happen, and it would be my fault because I didn’t check properly. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of something bad happening that drives you to check things repeatedly, or is it more about trying to get rid of the uncertainty in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m honest, the uncertainty is what keeps it going. The bad thing is what starts it in my head, like, “What if the outlet isn’t right?” or “What if the lock didn’t catch?” or “What if I left a tool somewhere and something happens?” But then even after I check, the real problem is I can’t get my mind to settle.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going back over the same things in the house—outlets, locks, tools, even little stuff like dust or whether something looks properly dealt with—and I check them, fix them, look at them again, and then I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I’ll walk away and it’s like there’s this little doubt still sitting there, and I can’t stand it, so I go back.\n\nAnd it wears me out. It’s not like I’m getting relief and moving on. I might feel a tiny bit better for a moment, but then the doubt comes back, and I’m doing the same thing again and again until I’m exhausted. So the thought of something bad happening matters, yes, but it’s that feeling of not being certain that really traps me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you believe you're more likely to make a mistake than others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m more careless than everyone else,” or that other people would definitely do it right and I wouldn’t. It’s more… I don’t trust that I personally caught everything in that moment.\n\nLike, even if another person might make the same mistake, my mind focuses on me being the one responsible. I’ll think, “What if I didn’t notice something?” or “What if I looked but didn’t really see it?” But I don’t know if that means I think I’m more likely to mess up than other people. It feels less like a comparison and more like I just can’t rely on my own certainty.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not exactly. I haven’t really noticed myself comparing my mistake rate to other people’s. It’s more that when I’m the one who checked it, I still doubt it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are more prone to errors or mistakes than the average person?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I believe that, exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m worse than the average person” or “I make more mistakes than everyone else.” That’s not really the wording that goes through my head.\n\nIt’s more that when I’m the one who did something, I can’t quite trust that I did it right enough. Like I can know, in a normal sense, that I’m not especially incompetent or anything, but then in the moment there’s this doubt about whether I missed something. So I guess I don’t really have a clear belief that I’m more error-prone than other people. I haven’t compared it that way much.\n\nIt’s more personal than statistical, if that makes sense. Like, “Can I be sure I didn’t miss anything?” not, “Do I make more mistakes than the average person?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things repeatedly helps you feel more in control of what might happen?\n\nPatient: In a way, yes, but it’s not a clean kind of control, if that makes sense. When I’m checking the outlets or the locks or the tools, or even dust and little things around the house, it feels like I’m trying to get some grip on what could go wrong. Like if I just look one more time, or fix it one more time, then maybe I can finally say, “Okay, that’s handled.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stay that way. I might feel like I’ve got a bit more control for a second, and then almost right away I start wondering if I checked properly, or if I missed something, or if it still isn’t dealt with enough. So I go back over the same things again and again. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—just looping around the house. It takes up most of my day, honestly, and by the end I’m worn out.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m trying to feel more in control, but the checking doesn’t actually make me feel settled for long. It just keeps me stuck doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking is ever driven by a desire to make your surroundings feel more orderly or symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes “orderly” is part of it, but I don’t know if I’d say symmetry exactly. It’s not usually like I need everything perfectly lined up because it looks nice or balanced. It’s more that if something looks off, or if I notice dust, or a tool isn’t where I think it should be, then it starts this whole thing where I feel like I have to fix it or check it again.\n\nThe bigger problem is I just keep going back over the same things in the house. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—again and again. I’ll check one thing, then move to another, then come back because I’m not sure I really checked the first one right, or I’ll see something that doesn’t feel finished. And then I’m fixing it, or wiping it, or checking it, and somehow hours have gone by.\n\nSo if you mean, do I do it to make the house feel more “right,” then yes, kind of. But it’s not satisfying. It doesn’t stay orderly in my head. I spend most of the day doing it until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive checking is because you believe you're not capable of doing things correctly the first time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I don’t think I walk around believing, “I can’t do anything right the first time.” It’s more like… once I’ve done something, I don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nLike with the outlets, locks, tools, or even dust around the house, I’ll check or fix something, and part of me knows I already did it. But then I start thinking, “Did I really look closely enough? Did I miss something? Is it actually okay?” And then I go back. And then I go back again. It’s not so much that I think I’m incapable, it’s that the feeling of being done doesn’t really stick.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going over the same things in the house again and again, checking and fixing them until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop and leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

The bad thing is what starts it in my head, like, “What if the outlet isn’t right?” or “What if the lock didn’t catch?” or “What if I left a tool somewhere and something happens?” But then even after I check, the real problem is I can’t get my mind to settle.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going back over the same things in the house—outlets, locks, tools, even little stuff like dust or whether something looks properly dealt with—and I check them, fix them, look at them again, and then I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I’ll walk away and it’s like there’s this little doubt still sitting there, and I can’t stand it, so I go back.\n\nAnd it wears me out. It’s not like I’m getting relief and moving on. I might feel a tiny bit better for a moment, but then the doubt comes back, and I’m doing the same thing again and again until I’m exhausted. So the thought of something bad happening matters, yes, but it’s that feeling of not being certain that really traps me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you believe you're more likely to make a mistake than others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m more careless than everyone else,” or that other people would definitely do it right and I wouldn’t. It’s more… I don’t trust that I personally caught everything in that moment.\n\nLike, even if another person might make the same mistake, my mind focuses on me being the one responsible. I’ll think, “What if I didn’t notice something?” or “What if I looked but didn’t really see it?” But I don’t know if that means I think I’m more likely to mess up than other people. It feels less like a comparison and more like I just can’t rely on my own certainty.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not exactly. I haven’t really noticed myself comparing my mistake rate to other people’s. It’s more that when I’m the one who checked it, I still doubt it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are more prone to errors or mistakes than the average person?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I believe that, exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m worse than the average person” or “I make more mistakes than everyone else.” That’s not really the wording that goes through my head.\n\nIt’s more that when I’m the one who did something, I can’t quite trust that I did it right enough. Like I can know, in a normal sense, that I’m not especially incompetent or anything, but then in the moment there’s this doubt about whether I missed something. So I guess I don’t really have a clear belief that I’m more error-prone than other people. I haven’t compared it that way much.\n\nIt’s more personal than statistical, if that makes sense. Like, “Can I be sure I didn’t miss anything?” not, “Do I make more mistakes than the average person?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things repeatedly helps you feel more in control of what might happen?\n\nPatient: In a way, yes, but it’s not a clean kind of control, if that makes sense. When I’m checking the outlets or the locks or the tools, or even dust and little things around the house, it feels like I’m trying to get some grip on what could go wrong. Like if I just look one more time, or fix it one more time, then maybe I can finally say, “Okay, that’s handled.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stay that way. I might feel like I’ve got a bit more control for a second, and then almost right away I start wondering if I checked properly, or if I missed something, or if it still isn’t dealt with enough. So I go back over the same things again and again. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—just looping around the house. It takes up most of my day, honestly, and by the end I’m worn out.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m trying to feel more in control, but the checking doesn’t actually make me feel settled for long. It just keeps me stuck doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking is ever driven by a desire to make your surroundings feel more orderly or symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes “orderly” is part of it, but I don’t know if I’d say symmetry exactly. It’s not usually like I need everything perfectly lined up because it looks nice or balanced. It’s more that if something looks off, or if I notice dust, or a tool isn’t where I think it should be, then it starts this whole thing where I feel like I have to fix it or check it again.\n\nThe bigger problem is I just keep going back over the same things in the house. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—again and again. I’ll check one thing, then move to another, then come back because I’m not sure I really checked the first one right, or I’ll see something that doesn’t feel finished. And then I’m fixing it, or wiping it, or checking it, and somehow hours have gone by.\n\nSo if you mean, do I do it to make the house feel more “right,” then yes, kind of. But it’s not satisfying. It doesn’t stay orderly in my head. I spend most of the day doing it until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive checking is because you believe you're not capable of doing things correctly the first time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I don’t think I walk around believing, “I can’t do anything right the first time.” It’s more like… once I’ve done something, I don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nLike with the outlets, locks, tools, or even dust around the house, I’ll check or fix something, and part of me knows I already did it. But then I start thinking, “Did I really look closely enough? Did I miss something? Is it actually okay?” And then I go back. And then I go back again. It’s not so much that I think I’m incapable, it’s that the feeling of being done doesn’t really stick.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going over the same things in the house again and again, checking and fixing them until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop and leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s exactly that I believe I’m not good enough, no. It’s not like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m bad at locking a door” or “I don’t know how to check an outlet.” I know, logically, these are ordinary things. I know I should be able to do them once and move on.

But when I actually try to leave it alone, it doesn’t feel finished. That’s the part I can’t seem to get past. I’ll check the lock, or the outlet, or whether a tool is put away right, or I’ll notice dust and feel like I have to fix it, and then even after I do it, there’s this doubt that comes right back in. Like maybe I didn’t check it closely enough, or maybe I only thought I fixed it, or maybe I should look again just to be sure.

So I end up spending most of my day going back over the same things in the house — outlets, locks, tools, dust — checking and fixing them again and again. It gets exhausting. I get worn out from it, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it’s less about thinking I’m not good enough, and more like I can’t get that sense of being done.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like my whole day gets swallowed up by checking things in the house. Same with the locks—I can look right at them, touch them, make sure they’re set, but then a few minutes later I’m back there again. Tools have to be put just right, and if there’s dust or something out of place, I’ll fix it, then fix it again, and it just keeps going.\n\nBy the time I’m done, I’m exhausted, but the worst part is I still don’t feel completely sure. It’s like I can’t trust that it’s okay enough to leave alone, even when I’ve already spent hours on it. That’s really why I came in. It’s taking over most of my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind and are disturbing or upsetting to you?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of what makes me go back and check, I think. It’s not just that I’m being careful. It’s these thoughts that jump into my head, and they feel awful.\n\nLike, I’ll know I looked at something properly. I’ll know I tightened something or put something away or checked the outlet, but then this thought comes in like, what if there’s a loose wire I missed and the whole house burns down? Or if I was using nails or tools, I’ll get this thought that maybe I left a nail somewhere and my wife is going to step on it or get hurt somehow because I was careless.\n\nI try to tell myself, no, I already checked, I did it right, but it doesn’t really stick. I try to push the thought away, and sometimes that almost makes it louder, like it comes back stronger and meaner. It keeps repeating until I feel like I have to do something about it, because if I don’t, I feel like I’m choosing to let something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. I’m not just checking for the sake of checking, even though I know from the outside it probably looks that way. In my head, I’m doing it because if I don’t, something could go wrong and it would be my fault.\n\nSo I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things around the house, and I keep checking them or fixing them because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. But the problem is it never really feels finished. I can check it, and then I’ll think, “Did I really see it? Did I miss something?” So I go back again.\n\nIt ends up taking most of my day sometimes. I get worn out from it, honestly, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but for me it’s less like everything has to look perfect and more like it has to feel settled in the right way. Like the tools can’t just be “put away,” they have to be in the spot where I can look at them and feel like, okay, nothing’s loose, nothing’s been left out, nothing’s going to cause a problem. And even then, a lot of the time I don’t get that feeling.\n\nI’ll go back over the same things again and again—the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little bits around the house—and I’ll check them or fix them, then stand there trying to decide if it’s okay enough. Sometimes I move something or wipe something and then I think, no, that still doesn’t seem right, or I’m not sure I really checked it. So I do it again.\n\nIt ends up taking up most of my day, honestly. I get worn out from going over the same places so many times, but even after all that I still don’t really feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be symmetrical or exactly in a certain position?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need one thing lined up perfectly with another thing, or everything has to look balanced. It’s more that I get stuck on whether it’s actually safe or done right.\n\nLike I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even little bits of dust or things around the house, and I’ll check them or fix them again and again. Sometimes something being a little out of place bothers me, but mostly because it makes me think I missed something, or that something isn’t secure, or that I didn’t really finish checking it properly.\n\nSo it can look like I’m arranging things, I guess, because I’ll move tools around or wipe an area or put something back a certain way, but I’m not trying to make it symmetrical. I’m trying to get that feeling of, “Okay, now I can leave it alone.” But I don’t really get there. I can spend most of the day going back over the same stuff until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to walk away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or fixing needs to be perfect, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: It’s more about preventing something bad from happening, I think. I mean, “perfect” is part of it in the sense that if it doesn’t feel right, I can’t leave it alone, but I’m not sitting there trying to make it look flawless or neat just for the sake of it.\n\nIt’s like… I’m here because I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust in the house, checking and fixing them again and again. I’ll think, okay, if I miss something, something could go wrong, or I’ll be responsible somehow. So I check it, then I start doubting whether I really checked it properly, and then I go back.\n\nBy the end of it I’m worn out, honestly. But even when I’m exhausted, I still don’t feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone. So it’s not really about wanting it perfect like decoration or order—it’s more like I’m trying to get enough certainty that nothing bad is going to happen, and I just can’t seem to get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check things repeatedly even if someone else, like a family member, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, even if somebody else said, “I already checked the lock,” or “the outlet’s fine,” it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might help for a second, maybe, but then I start thinking, what if they didn’t check it the way I would, or what if they missed the thing I’m worried about, or what if I didn’t actually see it myself.\n\nSo I’d still end up going back over it. The outlets, the locks, the tools, little dust or bits around the house… I know it probably sounds excessive, but I can spend most of my day checking and fixing the same things again and again. And even after I’ve done it so many times that I’m worn out, I still don’t get that sure feeling, like, “Okay, it’s safe, I can leave it alone now.”\n\nSo someone else checking doesn’t really stop it. I wish it did. But I usually still feel like I have to check it myself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or contamination when you're checking these things around the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, at least. I’m not usually thinking, like, “This is covered in germs,” or that I’m going to get sick from touching it. That doesn’t really feel like the main thing in my head.\n\nWhen I’m checking around the house, it’s more like I’m trying to make sure something is safe or finished or not going to cause a problem. If I notice dust or something small, I might get stuck on it, but I don’t know that I’d call it contamination. It’s more that it makes me wonder if I missed something or if the area wasn’t really dealt with properly.\n\nI mean, I do clean or wipe things sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m scared of germs exactly. It’s hard to explain because in the moment it just feels like something is “not right,” and then I have to go back to it. But germs specifically… no, I haven’t really noticed that being what I’m worried about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to perform these checks a specific number of times to feel assured?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific number, no. It’s not like I say, “I have to check the lock seven times,” and then I’m done. I wish it worked that way, honestly, because then at least there’d be an ending to it.\n\nIt’s more that I keep going back until I feel sure, but the problem is I don’t really get that sure feeling. So I’ll check an outlet, or a lock, or a tool, or something I noticed with dust or whatever in the house, and then I walk away and almost immediately start doubting it. Like, did I really look closely enough? Did I fix it right? Did I miss something? And then I’m back there again.\n\nSometimes I lose track of how many times I’ve done it because it’s just over and over. It can take up most of the day, and by the end I’m exhausted, but even then I still don’t feel fully assured enough to just leave it alone. So it’s not a number exactly—it’s more like I’m chasing this feeling that it’s finally okay, and I can’t seem to reach it.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking things repeatedly, is it more because you're afraid of making a mistake itself, or because you're afraid of what might happen as a result of that mistake?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of what might happen after, I think. I mean, I do hate the idea that I made a mistake, but the part that really grabs onto me is the consequence of it. Like even if I know, logically, that I checked it and I did the job right, these thoughts just jump in anyway.\n\nI’ll think, what if there’s a loose wire I somehow missed and it burns the house down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt? And it’s not like I want to think that. I try to push it away and tell myself, no, I already checked, it’s fine, but then it comes back even stronger, like my brain is saying, “Are you sure? Are you absolutely sure?”\n\nSo it’s not just, “Oh, I don’t want to be wrong.” It’s more like… if I’m wrong, something terrible could happen, and it would be my fault because I didn’t check properly. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of something bad happening that drives you to check things repeatedly, or is it more about trying to get rid of the uncertainty in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m honest, the uncertainty is what keeps it going. The bad thing is what starts it in my head, like, “What if the outlet isn’t right?” or “What if the lock didn’t catch?” or “What if I left a tool somewhere and something happens?” But then even after I check, the real problem is I can’t get my mind to settle.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going back over the same things in the house—outlets, locks, tools, even little stuff like dust or whether something looks properly dealt with—and I check them, fix them, look at them again, and then I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I’ll walk away and it’s like there’s this little doubt still sitting there, and I can’t stand it, so I go back.\n\nAnd it wears me out. It’s not like I’m getting relief and moving on. I might feel a tiny bit better for a moment, but then the doubt comes back, and I’m doing the same thing again and again until I’m exhausted. So the thought of something bad happening matters, yes, but it’s that feeling of not being certain that really traps me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you believe you're more likely to make a mistake than others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m more careless than everyone else,” or that other people would definitely do it right and I wouldn’t. It’s more… I don’t trust that I personally caught everything in that moment.\n\nLike, even if another person might make the same mistake, my mind focuses on me being the one responsible. I’ll think, “What if I didn’t notice something?” or “What if I looked but didn’t really see it?” But I don’t know if that means I think I’m more likely to mess up than other people. It feels less like a comparison and more like I just can’t rely on my own certainty.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not exactly. I haven’t really noticed myself comparing my mistake rate to other people’s. It’s more that when I’m the one who checked it, I still doubt it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are more prone to errors or mistakes than the average person?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I believe that, exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m worse than the average person” or “I make more mistakes than everyone else.” That’s not really the wording that goes through my head.\n\nIt’s more that when I’m the one who did something, I can’t quite trust that I did it right enough. Like I can know, in a normal sense, that I’m not especially incompetent or anything, but then in the moment there’s this doubt about whether I missed something. So I guess I don’t really have a clear belief that I’m more error-prone than other people. I haven’t compared it that way much.\n\nIt’s more personal than statistical, if that makes sense. Like, “Can I be sure I didn’t miss anything?” not, “Do I make more mistakes than the average person?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things repeatedly helps you feel more in control of what might happen?\n\nPatient: In a way, yes, but it’s not a clean kind of control, if that makes sense. When I’m checking the outlets or the locks or the tools, or even dust and little things around the house, it feels like I’m trying to get some grip on what could go wrong. Like if I just look one more time, or fix it one more time, then maybe I can finally say, “Okay, that’s handled.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stay that way. I might feel like I’ve got a bit more control for a second, and then almost right away I start wondering if I checked properly, or if I missed something, or if it still isn’t dealt with enough. So I go back over the same things again and again. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—just looping around the house. It takes up most of my day, honestly, and by the end I’m worn out.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m trying to feel more in control, but the checking doesn’t actually make me feel settled for long. It just keeps me stuck doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking is ever driven by a desire to make your surroundings feel more orderly or symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes “orderly” is part of it, but I don’t know if I’d say symmetry exactly. It’s not usually like I need everything perfectly lined up because it looks nice or balanced. It’s more that if something looks off, or if I notice dust, or a tool isn’t where I think it should be, then it starts this whole thing where I feel like I have to fix it or check it again.\n\nThe bigger problem is I just keep going back over the same things in the house. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—again and again. I’ll check one thing, then move to another, then come back because I’m not sure I really checked the first one right, or I’ll see something that doesn’t feel finished. And then I’m fixing it, or wiping it, or checking it, and somehow hours have gone by.\n\nSo if you mean, do I do it to make the house feel more “right,” then yes, kind of. But it’s not satisfying. It doesn’t stay orderly in my head. I spend most of the day doing it until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive checking is because you believe you're not capable of doing things correctly the first time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I don’t think I walk around believing, “I can’t do anything right the first time.” It’s more like… once I’ve done something, I don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nLike with the outlets, locks, tools, or even dust around the house, I’ll check or fix something, and part of me knows I already did it. But then I start thinking, “Did I really look closely enough? Did I miss something? Is it actually okay?” And then I go back. And then I go back again. It’s not so much that I think I’m incapable, it’s that the feeling of being done doesn’t really stick.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going over the same things in the house again and again, checking and fixing them until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you believe you're not good enough at doing them correctly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I believe I’m not good enough, no. It’s not like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m bad at locking a door” or “I don’t know how to check an outlet.” I know, logically, these are ordinary things. I know I should be able to do them once and move on.\n\nBut when I actually try to leave it alone, it doesn’t feel finished. That’s the part I can’t seem to get past. I’ll check the lock, or the outlet, or whether a tool is put away right, or I’ll notice dust and feel like I have to fix it, and then even after I do it, there’s this doubt that comes right back in. Like maybe I didn’t check it closely enough, or maybe I only thought I fixed it, or maybe I should look again just to be sure.\n\nSo I end up spending most of my day going back over the same things in the house — outlets, locks, tools, dust — checking and fixing them again and again. It gets exhausting. I get worn out from it, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it’s less about thinking I’m not good enough, and more like I can’t get that sense of being done.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… that’s a big part of it, I think. It’s not just that I want things neat or that I don’t trust myself in a general way. It’s more like these thoughts jump in, really ugly ones, like if I missed a loose wire somewhere, the house could burn down, and then it would be my fault because I didn’t check it right. Or I’ll think maybe I left a nail or some tool out and my wife could step on it or get hurt, and then I picture it like I caused it by not being careful enough.

And the frustrating thing is, a lot of the time I know I did the job right. I can remember checking it. I can tell myself, “You already looked, it’s fine.” But then the thought comes back anyway, like, “What if you missed the one thing that matters?” I try to push it out, or reason with it, but it doesn’t really go away. Sometimes it feels like the more I try not to think about it, the stronger it comes back.

So yes, I guess I am afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong. Not in a vague way, but in this very specific, intense way where my mind keeps showing me something terrible happening because I didn’t check one little thing enough. That’s what pulls me back to check again.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like my whole day gets swallowed up by checking things in the house. It’s the same stuff over and over—outlets, locks, tools, dust, little things that should be simple but somehow never feel settled.\n\nI’ll check an outlet, and then I’ll walk away, but almost right away I feel like I didn’t really check it properly, or I missed something, so I go back. Same with the locks—I can look right at them, touch them, make sure they’re set, but then a few minutes later I’m back there again. Tools have to be put just right, and if there’s dust or something out of place, I’ll fix it, then fix it again, and it just keeps going.\n\nBy the time I’m done, I’m exhausted, but the worst part is I still don’t feel completely sure. It’s like I can’t trust that it’s okay enough to leave alone, even when I’ve already spent hours on it. That’s really why I came in. It’s these thoughts that jump into my head, and they feel awful.\n\nLike, I’ll know I looked at something properly. It keeps repeating until I feel like I have to do something about it, because if I don’t, I feel like I’m choosing to let something bad happen.\n\nDoctor: Are these repeated checks you're doing intended to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. I’m not just checking for the sake of checking, even though I know from the outside it probably looks that way. In my head, I’m doing it because if I don’t, something could go wrong and it would be my fault.\n\nSo I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things around the house, and I keep checking them or fixing them because I’m trying to make sure nothing bad happens. But the problem is it never really feels finished. I can check it, and then I’ll think, “Did I really see it? Did I miss something?” So I go back again.\n\nIt ends up taking most of my day sometimes. I get worn out from it, honestly, but even after all that, I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be in a certain order or arranged in a specific way for you to feel comfortable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, but for me it’s less like everything has to look perfect and more like it has to feel settled in the right way. Like the tools can’t just be “put away,” they have to be in the spot where I can look at them and feel like, okay, nothing’s loose, nothing’s been left out, nothing’s going to cause a problem. And even then, a lot of the time I don’t get that feeling.\n\nI’ll go back over the same things again and again—the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little bits around the house—and I’ll check them or fix them, then stand there trying to decide if it’s okay enough. Sometimes I move something or wipe something and then I think, no, that still doesn’t seem right, or I’m not sure I really checked it. So I do it again.\n\nIt ends up taking up most of my day, honestly. I get worn out from going over the same places so many times, but even after all that I still don’t really feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the things you check or arrange need to be symmetrical or exactly in a certain position?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. It’s not like I need one thing lined up perfectly with another thing, or everything has to look balanced. It’s more that I get stuck on whether it’s actually safe or done right.\n\nLike I’ll go back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even little bits of dust or things around the house, and I’ll check them or fix them again and again. Sometimes something being a little out of place bothers me, but mostly because it makes me think I missed something, or that something isn’t secure, or that I didn’t really finish checking it properly.\n\nSo it can look like I’m arranging things, I guess, because I’ll move tools around or wipe an area or put something back a certain way, but I’m not trying to make it symmetrical. I’m trying to get that feeling of, “Okay, now I can leave it alone.” But I don’t really get there. I can spend most of the day going back over the same stuff until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to walk away from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking or fixing needs to be perfect, or is it more about preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: It’s more about preventing something bad from happening, I think. I mean, “perfect” is part of it in the sense that if it doesn’t feel right, I can’t leave it alone, but I’m not sitting there trying to make it look flawless or neat just for the sake of it.\n\nIt’s like… I’m here because I spend most of my day going back over the same outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust in the house, checking and fixing them again and again. I’ll think, okay, if I miss something, something could go wrong, or I’ll be responsible somehow. So I check it, then I start doubting whether I really checked it properly, and then I go back.\n\nBy the end of it I’m worn out, honestly. But even when I’m exhausted, I still don’t feel sure enough to just walk away and leave it alone. So it’s not really about wanting it perfect like decoration or order—it’s more like I’m trying to get enough certainty that nothing bad is going to happen, and I just can’t seem to get there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you would still check things repeatedly even if someone else, like a family member, had already checked them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, even if somebody else said, “I already checked the lock,” or “the outlet’s fine,” it doesn’t really settle it for me. It might help for a second, maybe, but then I start thinking, what if they didn’t check it the way I would, or what if they missed the thing I’m worried about, or what if I didn’t actually see it myself.\n\nSo I’d still end up going back over it. The outlets, the locks, the tools, little dust or bits around the house… I know it probably sounds excessive, but I can spend most of my day checking and fixing the same things again and again. And even after I’ve done it so many times that I’m worn out, I still don’t get that sure feeling, like, “Okay, it’s safe, I can leave it alone now.”\n\nSo someone else checking doesn’t really stop it. I wish it did. But I usually still feel like I have to check it myself.\n\nDoctor: Are you ever worried about germs or contamination when you're checking these things around the house?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, at least. I’m not usually thinking, like, “This is covered in germs,” or that I’m going to get sick from touching it. That doesn’t really feel like the main thing in my head.\n\nWhen I’m checking around the house, it’s more like I’m trying to make sure something is safe or finished or not going to cause a problem. If I notice dust or something small, I might get stuck on it, but I don’t know that I’d call it contamination. It’s more that it makes me wonder if I missed something or if the area wasn’t really dealt with properly.\n\nI mean, I do clean or wipe things sometimes, but I’m not sure it’s because I’m scared of germs exactly. It’s hard to explain because in the moment it just feels like something is “not right,” and then I have to go back to it. But germs specifically… no, I haven’t really noticed that being what I’m worried about.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to perform these checks a specific number of times to feel assured?\n\nPatient: Not really a specific number, no. It’s not like I say, “I have to check the lock seven times,” and then I’m done. I wish it worked that way, honestly, because then at least there’d be an ending to it.\n\nIt’s more that I keep going back until I feel sure, but the problem is I don’t really get that sure feeling. So I’ll check an outlet, or a lock, or a tool, or something I noticed with dust or whatever in the house, and then I walk away and almost immediately start doubting it. Like, did I really look closely enough? Did I fix it right? Did I miss something? And then I’m back there again.\n\nSometimes I lose track of how many times I’ve done it because it’s just over and over. It can take up most of the day, and by the end I’m exhausted, but even then I still don’t feel fully assured enough to just leave it alone. So it’s not a number exactly—it’s more like I’m chasing this feeling that it’s finally okay, and I can’t seem to reach it.\n\nDoctor: When you're checking things repeatedly, is it more because you're afraid of making a mistake itself, or because you're afraid of what might happen as a result of that mistake?\n\nPatient: It’s more because of what might happen after, I think. I mean, I do hate the idea that I made a mistake, but the part that really grabs onto me is the consequence of it. Like even if I know, logically, that I checked it and I did the job right, these thoughts just jump in anyway.\n\nI’ll think, what if there’s a loose wire I somehow missed and it burns the house down? Or what if I left a nail somewhere and my wife steps on it or gets hurt? And it’s not like I want to think that. I try to push it away and tell myself, no, I already checked, it’s fine, but then it comes back even stronger, like my brain is saying, “Are you sure? Are you absolutely sure?”\n\nSo it’s not just, “Oh, I don’t want to be wrong.” It’s more like… if I’m wrong, something terrible could happen, and it would be my fault because I didn’t check properly. That’s the part I can’t seem to let go of.\n\nDoctor: Is it the thought of something bad happening that drives you to check things repeatedly, or is it more about trying to get rid of the uncertainty in your mind?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but if I’m honest, the uncertainty is what keeps it going. The bad thing is what starts it in my head, like, “What if the outlet isn’t right?” or “What if the lock didn’t catch?” or “What if I left a tool somewhere and something happens?” But then even after I check, the real problem is I can’t get my mind to settle.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going back over the same things in the house—outlets, locks, tools, even little stuff like dust or whether something looks properly dealt with—and I check them, fix them, look at them again, and then I still don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone. I’ll walk away and it’s like there’s this little doubt still sitting there, and I can’t stand it, so I go back.\n\nAnd it wears me out. It’s not like I’m getting relief and moving on. I might feel a tiny bit better for a moment, but then the doubt comes back, and I’m doing the same thing again and again until I’m exhausted. So the thought of something bad happening matters, yes, but it’s that feeling of not being certain that really traps me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you believe you're more likely to make a mistake than others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m more careless than everyone else,” or that other people would definitely do it right and I wouldn’t. It’s more… I don’t trust that I personally caught everything in that moment.\n\nLike, even if another person might make the same mistake, my mind focuses on me being the one responsible. I’ll think, “What if I didn’t notice something?” or “What if I looked but didn’t really see it?” But I don’t know if that means I think I’m more likely to mess up than other people. It feels less like a comparison and more like I just can’t rely on my own certainty.\n\nSo I guess the answer is, not exactly. I haven’t really noticed myself comparing my mistake rate to other people’s. It’s more that when I’m the one who checked it, I still doubt it.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that you are more prone to errors or mistakes than the average person?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I believe that, exactly. I mean, I’m not sitting there thinking, “I’m worse than the average person” or “I make more mistakes than everyone else.” That’s not really the wording that goes through my head.\n\nIt’s more that when I’m the one who did something, I can’t quite trust that I did it right enough. Like I can know, in a normal sense, that I’m not especially incompetent or anything, but then in the moment there’s this doubt about whether I missed something. So I guess I don’t really have a clear belief that I’m more error-prone than other people. I haven’t compared it that way much.\n\nIt’s more personal than statistical, if that makes sense. Like, “Can I be sure I didn’t miss anything?” not, “Do I make more mistakes than the average person?”\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking these things repeatedly helps you feel more in control of what might happen?\n\nPatient: In a way, yes, but it’s not a clean kind of control, if that makes sense. When I’m checking the outlets or the locks or the tools, or even dust and little things around the house, it feels like I’m trying to get some grip on what could go wrong. Like if I just look one more time, or fix it one more time, then maybe I can finally say, “Okay, that’s handled.”\n\nBut it doesn’t really stay that way. I might feel like I’ve got a bit more control for a second, and then almost right away I start wondering if I checked properly, or if I missed something, or if it still isn’t dealt with enough. So I go back over the same things again and again. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—just looping around the house. It takes up most of my day, honestly, and by the end I’m worn out.\n\nSo yes, I think I’m trying to feel more in control, but the checking doesn’t actually make me feel settled for long. It just keeps me stuck doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive checking is ever driven by a desire to make your surroundings feel more orderly or symmetrical?\n\nPatient: I mean… sometimes “orderly” is part of it, but I don’t know if I’d say symmetry exactly. It’s not usually like I need everything perfectly lined up because it looks nice or balanced. It’s more that if something looks off, or if I notice dust, or a tool isn’t where I think it should be, then it starts this whole thing where I feel like I have to fix it or check it again.\n\nThe bigger problem is I just keep going back over the same things in the house. Outlets, locks, tools, dust—again and again. I’ll check one thing, then move to another, then come back because I’m not sure I really checked the first one right, or I’ll see something that doesn’t feel finished. And then I’m fixing it, or wiping it, or checking it, and somehow hours have gone by.\n\nSo if you mean, do I do it to make the house feel more “right,” then yes, kind of. But it’s not satisfying. It doesn’t stay orderly in my head. I spend most of the day doing it until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive checking is because you believe you're not capable of doing things correctly the first time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I don’t think I walk around believing, “I can’t do anything right the first time.” It’s more like… once I’ve done something, I don’t feel sure enough to leave it alone.\n\nLike with the outlets, locks, tools, or even dust around the house, I’ll check or fix something, and part of me knows I already did it. But then I start thinking, “Did I really look closely enough? Did I miss something? Is it actually okay?” And then I go back. And then I go back again. It’s not so much that I think I’m incapable, it’s that the feeling of being done doesn’t really stick.\n\nThat’s basically why I’m here. I spend most of my day going over the same things in the house again and again, checking and fixing them until I’m completely worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just stop and leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you believe you're not good enough at doing them correctly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s exactly that I believe I’m not good enough, no. It’s not like I’m standing there thinking, “I’m bad at locking a door” or “I don’t know how to check an outlet.” I know, logically, these are ordinary things. I know I should be able to do them once and move on.\n\nBut when I actually try to leave it alone, it doesn’t feel finished. That’s the part I can’t seem to get past. I’ll check the lock, or the outlet, or whether a tool is put away right, or I’ll notice dust and feel like I have to fix it, and then even after I do it, there’s this doubt that comes right back in. Like maybe I didn’t check it closely enough, or maybe I only thought I fixed it, or maybe I should look again just to be sure.\n\nSo I end up spending most of my day going back over the same things in the house — outlets, locks, tools, dust — checking and fixing them again and again. It gets exhausting. I get worn out from it, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it’s less about thinking I’m not good enough, and more like I can’t get that sense of being done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you're afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a big part of it, I think. It’s not just that I want things neat or that I don’t trust myself in a general way. It’s more like these thoughts jump in, really ugly ones, like if I missed a loose wire somewhere, the house could burn down, and then it would be my fault because I didn’t check it right. Or I’ll think maybe I left a nail or some tool out and my wife could step on it or get hurt, and then I picture it like I caused it by not being careful enough.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, a lot of the time I know I did the job right. I can remember checking it. I can tell myself, “You already looked, it’s fine.” But then the thought comes back anyway, like, “What if you missed the one thing that matters?” I try to push it out, or reason with it, but it doesn’t really go away. Sometimes it feels like the more I try not to think about it, the stronger it comes back.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong. Not in a vague way, but in this very specific, intense way where my mind keeps showing me something terrible happening because I didn’t check one little thing enough. That’s what pulls me back to check again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It gets exhausting. I get worn out from it, but even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone. So it’s less about thinking I’m not good enough, and more like I can’t get that sense of being done.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're checking things repeatedly because you're afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s a big part of it, I think. It’s not just that I want things neat or that I don’t trust myself in a general way. It’s more like these thoughts jump in, really ugly ones, like if I missed a loose wire somewhere, the house could burn down, and then it would be my fault because I didn’t check it right. Or I’ll think maybe I left a nail or some tool out and my wife could step on it or get hurt, and then I picture it like I caused it by not being careful enough.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, a lot of the time I know I did the job right. I can remember checking it. I can tell myself, “You already looked, it’s fine.” But then the thought comes back anyway, like, “What if you missed the one thing that matters?” I try to push it out, or reason with it, but it doesn’t really go away. Sometimes it feels like the more I try not to think about it, the stronger it comes back.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong. Not in a vague way, but in this very specific, intense way where my mind keeps showing me something terrible happening because I didn’t check one little thing enough. That’s what pulls me back to check again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like checking things repeatedly helps you avoid feeling guilty if something were to go wrong?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so… at least that’s how it feels in the moment. It’s like if I check the outlet again, or the lock again, or make sure the tools are put away right, then maybe I won’t have to sit with that horrible feeling that I should have done more. Like I’m trying to prevent not just something going wrong, but that feeling afterward of, “You knew you should have checked and you didn’t.”

But the problem is it doesn’t really work, not for long. I’ll go back over the same things in the house — the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things that look wrong — and I’ll check them or fix them, and for a second I might feel like, okay, maybe that’s enough. Then almost immediately I start doubting it again. So I go back. And then again. And it ends up taking over most of my day.

By the time I’m worn out, I’m not even feeling reassured exactly. I’m just exhausted from trying to get to a point where I can leave it alone without feeling like I’m risking something or setting myself up to feel guilty later. But I still don’t really get that solid feeling of, “It’s done, you can stop now.”

It’s more like these thoughts jump in, really ugly ones, like if I missed a loose wire somewhere, the house could burn down, and then it would be my fault because I didn’t check it right. Or I’ll think maybe I left a nail or some tool out and my wife could step on it or get hurt, and then I picture it like I caused it by not being careful enough.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is, a lot of the time I know I did the job right. I can remember checking it. I can tell myself, “You already looked, it’s fine.” But then the thought comes back anyway, like, “What if you missed the one thing that matters?” I try to push it out, or reason with it, but it doesn’t really go away. Sometimes it feels like the more I try not to think about it, the stronger it comes back.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong. Not in a vague way, but in this very specific, intense way where my mind keeps showing me something terrible happening because I didn’t check one little thing enough. That’s what pulls me back to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps you avoid feeling guilty if something were to go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least that’s how it feels in the moment. It’s like if I check the outlet again, or the lock again, or make sure the tools are put away right, then maybe I won’t have to sit with that horrible feeling that I should have done more. Like I’m trying to prevent not just something going wrong, but that feeling afterward of, “You knew you should have checked and you didn’t.”\n\nBut the problem is it doesn’t really work, not for long. I’ll go back over the same things in the house — the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things that look wrong — and I’ll check them or fix them, and for a second I might feel like, okay, maybe that’s enough. Then almost immediately I start doubting it again. So I go back. And then again. And it ends up taking over most of my day.\n\nBy the time I’m worn out, I’m not even feeling reassured exactly. I’m just exhausted from trying to get to a point where I can leave it alone without feeling like I’m risking something or setting myself up to feel guilty later. But I still don’t really get that solid feeling of, “It’s done, you can stop now.”\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll check one thing, then move to another, then come back because I’m not sure I really checked the first one right, or I’ll see something that doesn’t feel finished. And then I’m fixing it, or wiping it, or checking it, and somehow hours have gone by.\n\nSo if you mean, do I do it to make the house feel more “right,” then yes, kind of. But it’s not satisfying. It doesn’t stay orderly in my head. I spend most of the day doing it until I’m worn out, and even then I still don’t feel sure enough to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your repetitive checking is because you believe you're not capable of doing things correctly the first time?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I’ll check the lock, or the outlet, or whether a tool is put away right, or I’ll notice dust and feel like I have to fix it, and then even after I do it, there’s this doubt that comes right back in. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s part of it. I mean, when I’m going back over the outlets or the locks or making sure the tools are put away right, it does feel like I’m trying to stop something bad from happening to someone else, not just trying to make myself feel better.

That’s why I have such a hard time leaving it alone. I’ll check something, and then instead of feeling finished, my mind sort of grabs onto it again — like, what if that outlet wasn’t really okay, or what if the lock didn’t catch, or what if one of the tools is still somewhere it shouldn’t be. Even dust or little things around the house can start feeling like something I need to fix, like if I don’t deal with it then I’m being careless.

And then I end up spending most of the day going back over those same things again and again. It’s not one quick check. It becomes this whole loop where I check, fix, look again, doubt it, and go back. By the end I’m completely worn out, but I still don’t really get that sure feeling that it’s safe to leave it alone. So yes, I guess in my mind it does feel like I’m trying to protect people from something going wrong, even if I can’t always explain why checking one more time is supposed to make it certain.

Sometimes it feels like the more I try not to think about it, the stronger it comes back.\n\nSo yes, I guess I am afraid of being responsible if something goes wrong. Not in a vague way, but in this very specific, intense way where my mind keeps showing me something terrible happening because I didn’t check one little thing enough. That’s what pulls me back to check again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps you avoid feeling guilty if something were to go wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so… at least that’s how it feels in the moment. It’s like if I check the outlet again, or the lock again, or make sure the tools are put away right, then maybe I won’t have to sit with that horrible feeling that I should have done more. Like I’m trying to prevent not just something going wrong, but that feeling afterward of, “You knew you should have checked and you didn’t.”\n\nBut the problem is it doesn’t really work, not for long. I’ll go back over the same things in the house — the outlets, the locks, the tools, even dust or little things that look wrong — and I’ll check them or fix them, and for a second I might feel like, okay, maybe that’s enough. Then almost immediately I start doubting it again. So I go back. And then again. And it ends up taking over most of my day.\n\nBy the time I’m worn out, I’m not even feeling reassured exactly. I’m just exhausted from trying to get to a point where I can leave it alone without feeling like I’m risking something or setting myself up to feel guilty later. But I still don’t really get that solid feeling of, “It’s done, you can stop now.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're trying to protect others from potential harm?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I mean, when I’m going back over the outlets or the locks or making sure the tools are put away right, it does feel like I’m trying to stop something bad from happening to someone else, not just trying to make myself feel better.\n\nThat’s why I have such a hard time leaving it alone. By the end I’m completely worn out, but I still don’t really get that sure feeling that it’s safe to leave it alone. Then almost immediately I start doubting it again. So I go back. And then again. And it ends up taking over most of my day.\n\nBy the time I’m worn out, I’m not even feeling reassured exactly. I’m just exhausted from trying to get to a point where I can leave it alone without feeling like I’m risking something or setting myself up to feel guilty later. But I still don’t really get that solid feeling of, “It’s done, you can stop now.”\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check things repeatedly is because you're trying to protect others from potential harm?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s part of it. I mean, when I’m going back over the outlets or the locks or making sure the tools are put away right, it does feel like I’m trying to stop something bad from happening to someone else, not just trying to make myself feel better.\n\nThat’s why I have such a hard time leaving it alone. I’ll check something, and then instead of feeling finished, my mind sort of grabs onto it again — like, what if that outlet wasn’t really okay, or what if the lock didn’t catch, or what if one of the tools is still somewhere it shouldn’t be. So yes, I guess in my mind it does feel like I’m trying to protect people from something going wrong, even if I can’t always explain why checking one more time is supposed to make it certain."
